Explicit agency ratings were analysed using a one-factor repeated-measures ANOVA with objective outcome delays of 200, 400, and 600 ms as a within-subject factor. Estimated outcome delays were averaged separately within each participant, objective-delay condition, and task type. Estimated outcome delays were analysed using a two-factor repeated-measures ANOVA with objective outcome delay and task type (active vs. passive) as within-subject factors. Intentional binding was calculated as the difference between estimated outcome delays in active and passive trials (active minus passive), with more negative values representing stronger subjective temporal binding. These intentional binding scores were analysed using a one-factor repeated-measures ANOVA with objective outcome delay as a within-subject factor.

Perceived time compression at the trial level was additionally calculated as the difference between the participant's reported delay and the objective outcome delay, with more negative values representing stronger subjective temporal compression. To examine the correspondence between self-reported agency and perceived temporal compression, a trial-wise linear mixed-effects model was fitted with self-reported agency as the outcome and perceived temporal compression as the predictor across all five experimental conditions.

To assess differences between experimental conditions, self-reported agency and perceived temporal compression were separately analysed using linear mixed-effects models with condition as a fixed effect. To examine the effects of aesthetic appearance and motor autonomy, additional models included these factors and their interaction as fixed effects, considering only active conditions. The same factorial structure was used to analyse prosthesis embodiment scores and object repositioning accuracy, with embodiment scores analysed at the participant-condition level.

For the analysis of the exclusion rates across active experimental conditions, trial exclusion rates were calculated at the participant level as the percentage of invalid trials within each of the active conditions: HF, HS, RF, and RS. These exclusion rates were analysed using a linear mixed-effects model with aesthetic appearance (Hannes vs. realistic) and motor autonomy (with vs. without slippage compensation) as fixed effects and participant as a random intercept. Separate linear mixed-effects models were fitted for prosthesis, cylinder, and goal-area dwell times across active conditions, with self-reported agency as a fixed effect and participant as a random intercept to analyse the relationship between self-reported agency and visual monitoring. Dwell times were expressed as absolute durations in seconds.

Because nine participants contributed no valid trials in any active condition, they could not be included in analyses requiring the aesthetic appearance × motor autonomy factorial design, as the conditions analysed included only active trials. Consequently, these analyses were restricted to 48 participants. This restriction reflected the absence of valid active trials rather than a requirement for complete data across all four active conditions. Similarly, One-sample tests included participants with available activepassive differences at each outcome delay, resulting in sample sizes of 43, 44, and 42 at 200, 400, and 600 ms, respectively. The repeated-measures analyses of estimated delays and intentional binding included 37 participants with complete observations across all required task-type and delay combinations. Analyses including passive trials retained up to 57 participants. Appendix A provides an in-depth description and replication of the entire Results section using the subset of 48 participants with at least one valid trial in an active condition, with no significant deviations reported.

4. Results

>Results >Intentional binding

4.1.1. Self-reported agency and perceived temporal compression

A linear mixed-effects model was fitted with a random intercept for participant to assess the relationship between perceived temporal compression (implicit) and self-reported agency (explicit) measures across all conditions. Perceived temporal compression was computed as reported delay -outcome delay, with negative values indicating a stronger perceived time compression. The analysis revealed a significant effect of perceived temporal compression on self-reported agency (β = -0.030, SE = 0.002, t(2421) = -14.72, p < .001). Specifically, the reported agency increased by approximately 1% for every 33millisecond decrease in perceived temporal compression.

The model intercept was 44.28 (SE = 2.21, t(55.3) = 20.01, p < .001), representing the predicted self-reported agency score when perceived temporal compression was equal to zero. Between-participant variability (SD = 16.42) and residual variability (SD = 17.78) at the observation level were of similar magnitude. A graphical representation of the correlation between explicit and implicit sense of agency measures is shown in Figure 4.

4.1.2. Effects of outcome delay on self-reported agency, perceived temporal compression, and intentional binding

A repeated-measures ANOVA showed a significant effect of outcome delay on selfreported (explicit) agency ratings across all conditions (F(1.32, 73.96) = 23.05, p < .001, ηp² = .292). Post hoc comparisons indicated that (explicit) self-reported agency ratings were significantly higher at 200 ms than at 400 ms (t(56) = 5.20, p < .001) and at 600 ms (t(56) = 5.11, p < .001). Ratings were also significantly higher at 400 ms than at 600 ms (t(56) = 3.09, p = .009). A Graphical representation of the correlation between explicit sense of agency and outcome delay is shown in Figure 5A.

For the analysis of estimated delay, a repeated-measures ANOVA was performed and revealed significant main effects of outcome delay (F(1.92, 69.02) = 316.59, p < .001, ηp² = .898), and task type (active: HF, HS, RF, RS; or passive: P; F(1, 36) = 40.29, p < .001, ηp² = .528). A significant interaction was also found between outcome delay and task type (F(1.79, 64.39) = 3.54, p = .039, ηp² = .090). Pairwise comparisons with Bonferroni correction showed significant differences between estimated delays across all outcomedelay levels, with significant differences between the 200 ms and 400 ms (t(36) = -12.15, p < .001), the 200 ms and 600 ms (t(36) = -24.06, p < .001), and the 400 ms and 600 ms (t(36) = -13.86, p < .001). Estimated delays were significantly lower in the active rather than in the passive task within each outcome-delay condition: 200 ms (t(36) = -5.15, p < .001), 400 ms (t(36) = -3.57, p = .001), and 600 ms (t(36) = -6.53, p < .001). A graphical representation of the relationship between estimated and objective delays across active and passive tasks is shown in Figure 5B Supplementary one-sample tests using all available active-passive differences at each delay showed that intentional binding scores significantly differed from zero at all outcome-delay levels: 200 ms (M = -84.6 ms, t(42) = -5.56, p < .001, d = -0.85), 400 ms (M = -107 ms, t(43) = -4.42, p < .001, d = -0.67), and 600 ms (M = -142 ms, t(41) = -7.34, p < .001, d = -1.13). To further characterise the interaction between task type and outcome delay, pairwise comparisons of intentional binding scores between outcome delays were performed with Bonferroni correction. These comparisons showed a significant difference between 200 ms and 600 ms (t(36) = 2.78, p = .026), whereas the differences between 200 ms and 400 ms (t(36) = 0.07, p = 1.000) and between 400 ms and 600 ms (t(36) = 1.99, p = .164) were not significant. A graphical representation of intentional binding scores as a function of outcome delay is shown in Figure 5C.

4.2. Effects of experimental conditions on agency, embodiment, and task performance

>Effects of experimental conditions on agency, embodiment, and task performance

4.2.1. Condition by agency

A linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine the effect of condition on selfreported agency (explicit), with participant included as a random intercept. The reference level was the passive condition (P), and it was based on 2,454 observations from 57 participants. Between-participant variability (SD = 16.22) and residual variability at the trial level (SD = 16.82) were of similar magnitude. Compared with P, self-reported agency ratings were higher in HF (β = 15.00, SE = 1.23, t(2414) = 12.20, p < .001), HS (β = 19.05, SE = 1.06, t(2420) = 18.02, p < .001), RF (β = 17.13, SE = 1.26, t(2412) = 13.62, p < .001), and RS (β = 19.56, SE = 1.10, t(2417) = 17.84, p < .001). Estimated marginal means for each condition were 37.6 (SE = 2.20) for P, 52.6 (SE = 2.43) for HF, 56.6 (SE = 2.34) for HS, 54.7 (SE = 2.44) for RF, and 57.1 (SE = 2.36) for RS. Pairwise comparisons with Bonferroni correction showed significant differences among the active conditions. Selfreported agency ratings were lower in HF than in HS (mean difference = -4.05, SE = 1.35, t(2397) = -3.00, p = .027) and in RS (mean difference = -4.56, SE = 1.39, t(2398) = -3.27, p = .011). No other comparisons showed significant differences between active conditions.

To further characterise the effect of motor autonomy and aesthetic appearance on the sense of agency, a linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine their effects and their interaction on self-reported agency, with participant included as a random intercept. The model was based on 1,278 observations from 48 participants. Betweenparticipant variability (SD = 14.70) and residual variability (SD = 16.43) were of similar magnitude. The analysis showed a significant effect of motor autonomy. Relative to the condition without slippage compensation (HF and RF), slippage compensation (HS and RS) was associated with higher agency ratings (β = 3.39, SE = 1.34, t(1247) = 2.52, p = .012). In contrast, there was no significant effect of aesthetic appearance (β = 0.58, SE = 1.51, t(1244) = 0.38, p = .702). The interaction between aesthetic appearance and autonomy was also not significant (β = -0.96, SE = 1.93, t(1240) = -0.50, p = .620). A Graphical representation of self-reported agency values for each condition is shown in Figure 6. A linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine the effect of condition on perceived temporal compression , based on 2,454 observations from 57 participants. Betweenparticipant variability (SD = 74.99) was smaller than residual variability at the trial level (SD = 173.05). Relative to P, perceived temporal compression was lower in HF (β = -114.24, SE = 12.56, t(2448) = -9.10, p < .001), HS (β = -123.83, SE = 10.78, t(2447) = -11.50, p < .001), RF (β = -85.45, SE = 12.85, t(2447) = -6.65, p < .001), and RS (β = -113.74, SE = 11.19, t(2449) = -10.18, p < .001). The estimated mean perceived temporal compression score in the passive condition was 36.99 ms (SE = 11.14). Pairwise comparisons with Bonferroni-correction showed that the passive condition differed significantly from each active condition: HF (mean difference = 114.24 ms, SE = 12.56, t(2448) = 9.09, p < .001), HS (mean difference = 123.83 ms, SE = 10.78, t(2447) = 11.49, p < .001), RF (mean difference = 85.45 ms, SE = 12.85, t(2447) = 6.65, p < .001), and RS (mean difference = 113.74 ms, SE = 11.19, t(2449) = 10.17, p < .001). No other significant differences were observed among active conditions.

A linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine the effects of aesthetic appearance, motor autonomy, and their interaction on perceived temporal compression, with participant included as a random intercept. The model was based on 1,278 observations from 48 participants. Substantial variability was observed both between participants (SD = 81.48) and at the trial level (SD = 167.60). No significant effect of motor autonomy on perceived temporal compression was found (β = -4.42 ms, SE = 13.63, t(1264) = -0.32, p = .746). The interaction between aesthetic appearance and motor autonomy was also not significant (β = -20.69 ms, SE = 19.57, t(1256) = -1.06, p = .291). The effect of aesthetic appearance showed a trend, with the Human-like embodiment showing less negative perceived temporal compression scores than the Hannes embodiment (β = 29.16 ms, SE = 15.33, t(1262) = 1.90, p = .057). Estimated marginal means were -69.5 ms (SE = 16.8) for HF, -40.3 ms (SE = 17.0) for RF, -73.9 ms (SE = 15.6) for HS, and -65.4 ms (SE = 15.9) for RS. A Graphical representation of perceived temporal compression scores for each condition is shown in Figure 6 (right panel).

4.2.2. Differences in sense of embodiment

A linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine the effects of aesthetic appearance, motor autonomy, and their interaction on prosthesis embodiment scores (PEmbS-ULA scores in Rasch logits), with participant included as a random intercept. The model was based on 151 observations from 48 participants. Between-participant variability (SD = 1.13) and residual variability (SD = 1.23) were of similar magnitude. Using the Hannes-Friction condition as the reference level, the effect of aesthetic appearance was not found significant (β = 0.07, SE = 0.29, t(110) = 0.23, p = .815), nor the effect of motor autonomy (β = 0.13, SE = 0.30, t(112) = 0.43, p = .670), northe interaction between aesthetic appearance and motor autonomy (β = -0.10, SE = 0.41, t(109) = -0.24, p = .811). Estimated marginal means were 0.23 (SE = 0.27) for HF, 0.30 (SE = 0.26) for RF, 0.36 (SE = 0.26) for HS, and 0.33 (SE = 0.26) for RS. A Graphical representation of Prosthesis Embodiment scores in the Rasch logit scale for each active condition is shown in Figure 7.

4.2.3. Differences in performance

A linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine the effects of aesthetic appearance, motor autonomy, and their interaction on the number of failed trials, with participant included as a random intercept. The model was based on 228 observations from 57 participants (four active conditions per participant). Between-participant variability was bigger (SD = 26.43) than residual variability at the condition level (SD = 15.11). Using the HF condition as the reference level, failed trials were significantly lower in the conditions with slippage compensation (β = -11.95, SE = 2.83, t(168) = -4.22, p < .001). In contrast, no significant effect of aesthetic appearance was found (β = 1.50, SE = 2.83, t(168) = 0.53, p = .596), nor for the interaction between aesthetic appearance and autonomy (β = 2.34, SE = 4.00, t(168) = 0.58, p = .560). Estimated marginal means indicated trial exclusion rates of 77.9% (SE = 4.03) for HF, 79.4% (SE = 4.03) for RF, 66.0% (SE = 4.03) for HS, and 69.8% (SE = 4.03) for RS.

Pairwise comparisons with Bonferroni correction showed fewer significant failed trials in both conditions with slippage compensation compared with conditions without slippage compensation. Specifically, exclusion trial rates were found to be lower in HS than in HF (mean difference = 11.95%, SE = 2.83, t(168) = 4.22, p < .001) and RF (mean difference = 13.45%, SE = 2.83, t(168) = 4.75, p < .001). Similarly, exclusion rates were found to be lower in RS than in HF (mean difference = 8.10%, SE = 2.83, t(168) = 2.86, p = .028) and RF (mean difference = 9.61%, SE = 2.83, t(168) = 3.39, p = .005). No significant differences were found between the two conditions with slippage compensation (p = 1.00) or between the two conditions without slippage compensation (p = 1.00). A Graphical representation of the number of failed trials per active condition is shown in Figure 8. A linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine the effects of aesthetic appearance, motor autonomy, and their interaction on object repositioning accuracy in successfully performed trials, with participant included as a random intercept. The model was based on 1,278 observations from 48 participants. Between-participant variability was smaller (SD = 5.77) relative to residual variability at the trial level (SD = 13.04). Using the condition HF as the reference level, no significant effect was found of aesthetic appearance on repositioning accuracy (β = -1.01, SE = 1.19, t(1264) = -0.85, p = .398). The effect of motor autonomy showed a trend toward higher accuracy under the conditions with slippage compensation than in those without slippage compensation (β = 1.88, SE = 1.06, t(1266) = 1.77, p = .076). The interaction between aesthetic appearance and motor autonomy was not significant (β = -1.61, SE = 1.52, t(1258) = -1.06, p = .290). Estimated marginal means were 79.5% (SE = 1.24) for HF, 78.5% (SE = 1.26) for RF, 81.4% (SE = 1.15) for HS, and 78.8% (SE = 1.17) for RS.

Pairwise comparisons with Bonferroni correction showed significant differences in the repositioning accuracy in successful trials, with higher accuracy values in the HS condition than in the RF condition (mean difference = 2.89 percentage points, SE = 1.08, t(1256) = 2.68, p = .045). In addition, accuracy was higher in the HS condition than in the RS condition (mean difference = 2.62 percentage points, SE = 0.96, t(1256) = 2.73, p = .038). No other pairwise comparisons showed significance. A Graphical representation of the repositioning accuracy per active condition is shown in Figure 8.

4.3. Gaze data

>Gaze data

4.3.1. Prosthesis

A linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine the relationship between selfreported agency and prosthesis dwell time, with participant included as a random intercept. The model was based on 1,278 observations from 48 participants. Variability was observed both between participants (SD = 0.23) and at the observation level (SD = 0.39). Self-reported agency was found to significantly predict prosthesis dwell time (β = -0.0014, SE = 0.0007, t(1233) = -2.13, p = .034), with higher agency ratings being associated with shorter prosthesis dwell times. The model intercept was 0.556 (SE = 0.053, t(106) = 10.47, p < .001), indicating the predicted dwell time for self-reported agency was equal to zero. Across all active-condition observations, the relationship between reported agency and prosthesis dwell time was negative, with an estimated slope of -0.0014. This indicates a decrease of approximately 1.4 milliseconds in prosthesis dwell time for every one-percentage-point increase in self-reported agency.

A Graphical representation of the correlation between prosthesis dwell time and selfreported agency in the active conditions is shown in Figure 9.

4.3.2. Task object

A linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine the relationship between selfreported agency and cylinder dwell time, with participant included as a random intercept. The model was based on 1,278 observations from 48 participants. Variability was observed both between participants (SD = 0.52) and at the observation level (SD = 0.59). Self-reported agency significantly predicted cylinder dwell time (β = -0.0024, SE = 0.0010, t(1276) = -2.38, p = .017), with higher agency ratings associated with shorter cylinder dwell times. The model intercept was 1.64 (SE = 0.10, t(84.2) = 16.63, p < .001), indicating the predicted dwell time when reported agency was equal to zero. Across all active-condition observations, the relationship between self-reported agency and cylinder dwell time was negative, with an estimated slope of -0.0024. This indicates a decrease of approximately 2.4 milliseconds in cylinder dwell time for every onepercentage increase in reported agency. A Graphical representation of the correlation between cylinder dwell time and self-reported agency in the active conditions is shown in Figure 10.

4.3.3. Goal area

A linear mixed-effects model was fitted to examine the relationship between selfreported agency and goal area dwell time, with participant included as a random intercept. The model was based on 1,278 observations from 48 participants. Variability was observed both between participants (SD = 0.18) and at the observation level (SD = 0.23). Self-reported agency was not a significant predictor of goal area dwell time, although a negative trend was observed (β = -0.0007, SE = 0.0004, t(1274) = -1.80, p = .072). The model intercept was 0.221 (SE = 0.036, t(117) = 6.08, p < .001), representing the predicted goal area dwell time when self-reported agency was equal to zero.

5. Discussion

Sharing motor autonomy with a prosthesis capable of inferring the participant's intended action was associated with higher self-reported agency and fewer failed repositioning attempts, but it did not significantly affect perceived temporal compression or prosthesis embodiment scores. The device's aesthetic appearance did not significantly affect or moderate sense of agency measures, nor did it significantly affect prosthesis embodiment. Additionally, shorter outcome delays were associated with higher self-reported agency, whereas intentional binding increased in absolute magnitude at longer delays. Lastly, higher self-reported agency was associated with shorter dwell times over the prosthesis and the object being manipulated.

The analysis of perceived temporal compression and self-reported agency measures found a significant correlation between explicit and implicit measures of the sense of agency. This finding is consistent with previous studies reporting associations between explicit judgments of agency and intentional binding measures (Ebert & Wegner, 2010; Imaizumi & Tanno, 2019; Schofield et al., 2019). To the best of our knowledge, only a limited number of studies have investigated the sense of agency over upper-limb prostheses using an implicit agency paradigm, with Schofield et al. (2019) providing the most directly comparable example. In contrast to the open-ended temporal estimates used in previous work (Schofield et al., 2019), the present study employed a fixedresponse time estimation paradigm, supporting the use of this paradigm for the measurement of implicit sense of agency in tasks employing an upper limb prosthesis.

Additionally, the analysis of the effect of objective outcome delay (200 ms, 400 ms, and 600 ms) on self-reported agency revealed significant differences across all delay conditions, with higher agency ratings associated with shorter delays. This decrease in explicitly reported agency in the present study is consistent with previous research demonstrating that the sense of agency decreases as the temporal interval between an action and its outcome increases (Erdoğan & Balcı, 2025; Imaizumi & Tanno, 2019; Kim & Yoshida, 2024). Such effects have been attributed to a weakening of the perceived causal relationship between an action and its consequence as temporal contiguity decreases, reducing the likelihood that the outcome is attributed to one's own action (Wen & Imamizu, 2022).

Similarly, significant intentional binding effects were observed at all delay levels, indicating the presence of intentional binding at delays longer than those commonly observed in previous studies (Imaizumi & Tanno, 2019; Zapparoli et al., 2020). This was not expected and may relate to the embodied nature and temporal structure of our task. Unlike the discrete action used by Zapparoli et al. (2020), the present task involved a prolonged sequence of grasping, transporting, and releasing an object, accompanied by continuous visuomotor feedback. These features may have contributed to the observed binding pattern, although the present design does not allow their individual contributions to be isolated. However, the magnitude of intentional binding increased in absolute duration in longer delays, with the strongest effect observed at 600 ms. Notably, this pattern contrasted with the explicit agency ratings, which decreased as outcome delay increased. Thus, increasing action-outcome delays produced opposite effects on explicit and implicit measures of agency. This dissociation may reflect differences in the processes underlying the two measures: explicit judgments may rely more strongly on conscious causal attribution, whereas intentional binding may be influenced by temporal prediction and retrospective outcome processing. This interpretation is consistent with the evidence suggesting that the two measures may capture partially distinct aspects of agency processing (Ciaunica et al., 2024; Erdoğan & Balcı, 2025; Reddy, 2022). However, we cannot discard that the increase in the intentional binding effect as outcome delay increased may also have been influenced by Weber's law, which states that the just noticeable difference between two intervals is known to be proportional to the standard interval (Erdoğan & Balcı, 2025). A possible explanation would be that the use of a fixed 200 ms step difference between adjacent delays, together with the decrease in perceptual discrimination with delay magnitude, may have increased the likelihood that participants perceived delays as differing by more than a single interval. Under this explanation, reduced temporal discrimination would need to interact with agency-related perceived temporal compression differently in active and passive trials to account for the increasing difference between them as objective delay increased.

Although this explanation remains speculative, future studies should address the exploration of how the use of constant, linearly spaced delays in complex motor tasks interacts with delay magnitude and outcome expectations, both of which have been associated with effects on subjective time perception (Imaizumi & Tanno, 2019; Mariano et al., 2025; Tanaka et al., 2019).

Analysis of the effect of sharing the motor autonomy of the prosthetic device with an intention-detection algorithm indicated a positive effect on both self-reported (explicit) sense of agency over the device and the reduction in the number of failed repositioning attempts, with a trend toward improved placement accuracy. These findings support our hypothesis that an intention-detection algorithm could reduce the number of grasping failures while using a prosthetic device. Previous work has linked increased grasping failures to reduced explicit agency (Egle et al., 2026), suggesting that the reduced failure rate observed in this study may have contributed to the increase in agency ratings. Shared autonomy may therefore enhance agency when it supports the intended action without removing the user's contribution to its execution. Future studies could examine this hypothesis by testing whether grasping failure rates mediate the effect of shared motor autonomy on explicit agency.

Interestingly, no differences in prosthesis embodiment scores were found among conditions. Although this result does not support a direct relationship between the increased sense of agency and greater device embodiment, it suggests that agency alone may not be sufficient to produce a broader experience of embodiment. Other components, such as ownership, anatomical plausibility, and multisensory integration, may require longer exposure to the device. Nevertheless, these results need to be interpreted with caution, as the PEmbS-ULA scale was developed to assess the clinical population with amputation rather than the able-bodied population. Therefore, it is possible that embodiment processes in able-bodied participants differ from those in individuals with limb loss. In particular, previous work on the Rubber Hand Illusion has shown that embodiment of an artificial limb can be accompanied by partial disownership of the biological limb (della Gatta et al., 2016), potentially complicating the interpretation of embodiment scores in able-bodied samples. Additionally, our study design limits each virtual avatar's use to a few minutes within a constrained interaction environment, potentially limiting the integration of the prosthesis as part of the bodily self.

However, despite a clear difference in the magnitude of perceived temporal compression between the active and passive conditions, motor autonomy did not significantly affect the implicit measure of agency. In contrast, self-reported agency ratings were significantly influenced by motor autonomy. This dissociation suggests that implicit and explicit measures of agency may be differentially sensitive to changes in prosthesis autonomy, consistent with previous evidence showing that the two measures can respond differently depending on the task and experimental context (Ciaunica et al., 2024; Imaizumi & Tanno, 2019).

Additionally, the aesthetic appearance of the device did not significantly affect or moderate the explicit or the implicit sense of agency, nor prosthesis embodiment. These findings suggest that, within the context of the present task, functional aspects of the interaction may have had a greater influence than visual appearance. Indeed, because both avatars provided comparable visuomotor correspondence, differences in appearance may have been less relevant than the reliability of action execution. However, it would be necessary to further explore this effect in the population of prosthetic users, as personalisation and human-likeness of prosthetic devices have been found to be relevant for their embodiment and acceptance (Bekrater-Bodmann, 2020 , 2021; Caldas et al., 2026). The virtual setting may also have contributed to the limited influence of aesthetic appearance observed in this study. Previous studies have shown ownership of virtual limbs with substantially altered proportions when congruent sensorimotor feedback was provided (Kilteni et al., 2012), and found that movement congruency influenced intentional binding regardless of whether the controlled virtual object resembled a hand (Zopf et al., 2018), which may support the idea that consistent sensorimotor feedback can support aspects of agency and embodiment despite visual differences.

Lastly, the analysis of the gaze behaviour showed a decrease in the dwell times over the prosthesis and task object as the sense of agency increased. These results support our hypothesis that a higher sense of agency over the device would be associated with reduced visual monitoring of it. Evidence supporting this claim has also been found in terms of trust, reliability, and sense of control over the prosthetic device (Cheng et al., 2025; Egle et al., 2026).

Limitations

It is important to acknowledge that the findings of this study are constrained by several limitations. First, our experiment was conducted on a sample of able-bodied young adults. Although direct evidence comparing prosthesis embodiment between ablebodied and limb-missing individuals remains limited, sense of embodiment is generally considered to be mediated by the internal representation of the limb (Bekrater-Bodmann, 2020), which may undergo changes following amputation (Makin & Flor, 2020).

Second, movements of the virtual prosthesis were controlled using kinematic information obtained by optical hand tracking rather than from electromyography (EMG) signals. EMG control in polyarticulated prosthetic devices is more prone to movement misclassifications (Alotaibi et al., 2023), what has been associated with a decrease in the trust in, and sense of agency over, prosthetic devices (Egle et al., 2026). Additionally, the simulated intention-detection algorithm used represents an ideal system, as it had direct access to the participant's real hand state to adapt its behaviour, potentially increasing its accuracy with respect to a real inference algorithm.

Third, prosthesis embodiment was assessed using the PEmbS-ULA, a questionnaire validated for individuals with an upper-limb amputation. Although it provides a useful framework for evaluating sense of embodiment, its psychometric properties have not been established for able-bodied participants interacting with virtual prostheses.

Lastly, this study was conducted in a single session inside a VR environment and, as a result, it may not capture longer-term changes in embodiment arising from prolonged prosthesis use, nor from the haptic experiences associated with interacting with physical prosthetic devices in complex everyday environments. Future studies should aim to mitigate the limitations of this study and further validate its results across different tasks outside a VR environment using real physical prosthetic devices.

6. Conclusions

In conclusion, our study explored factors influencing the sense of agency and embodiment of a virtual prosthesis in a VR environment. The results showed that sharing autonomy with an ideal intention-detection algorithm can enhance self-reported agency while improving task performance by reducing grasping failures. Despite the correlation between explicit and implicit measures of agency, they responded differently to outcome delay and motor autonomy, supporting the view that they capture related yet partially distinct aspects of the sense of agency. Contrary to our expectations, motor autonomy and the prosthesis's aesthetic appearance did not significantly affect embodiment, suggesting that functional aspects of the interaction may have a greater influence than visual appearance during short-term use in our population. Finally, the observed association between higher self-reported agency and reduced visual attention toward the prosthesis and manipulated object provided further insight into the effect of agency over the prosthesis on gaze behaviour. Together, these findings contribute to our understanding of the sense of agency in prosthetic devices and highlight the potential of shared-autonomy approaches to improve the experience of upper-limb prosthesis users, warranting further investigation in clinical populations.

Figure 1.

Figure 1. Illustration of the virtual arm avatars within the virtual environment: prosthetic simulation (i.e., Hannes Hand) avatar (on the left side of the figure) and Realistic arm avatar (on the right side of the figure)were exclusively worn on the right arm, while a free-moving realistic avatar was consistently employed on the left arm to enhance participant immersion in the virtual environment. In both depictions, the virtual environment features a table, a central black box with a white button, an area for the object on the right

Figure 2.

Figure2. Graphical summary of the experimental task. The task would be composed of seven blocks: two for training in sequence (Passive and Active) and five experimental blocks in a pseudo-randomised order (realistic arm with slippage compensation (RS), realistic arm without slippage compensation (RF), Hannes Hand with slippage compensation (HS), Hannes Hand without slippage compensation (HF), and passive condition (P)). The training blocks consisted of 25 trials each with sound delays of 1, 200, 400, 600, and 800 milliseconds, and feedback on the delay was provided to the participant. The experimental blocks 24 trials with sound delays of 200, 400, and 600 milliseconds, and participants were asked for their time estimation of the delay and their sense of agency over it. Additionally, the questionnaire Prosthesis Embodiment Scale for Upper Limb Amputees (PEmbS-ULA) was administered after each experimental block.

Figure 3.

Figure3. Graphical representation of the panel providing feedback about the delay's duration during the training blocks (left of the black line) and of the panels used during the experimental blocks (right of the black line) to estimate the duration of the delay (centre) and their level of self-reported agency (right). Please note that the delays during the training blocks were 1, 200, 400, 600, and 800 milliseconds, while the delays during the experimental blocks were of 200, 400, and 600 milliseconds. Since participants were not told about this manipulation, all five delay options (1, 200, 400, 600, and 800 milliseconds) were available, allowing participants to select the one they perceived as appropriate if their temporal perception was influenced by the intentional binding effect.

Figure 4.

Figure 4. Graphical representation of the relationship between the explicit (self-reported) and implicit (perceived temporal compression) sense of agency across all conditions. More negative perceived temporal compression values indicate a stronger subjective time compression. The thick black line indicates the estimated marginal mean slope, and the shaded region the 95% CI. The small dots indicate the distribution of the data points for each participant and trial, with horizontal jittering for visualisation. Estimated values for the slope and p-value are indicated in the top-right corner of the figure.

Figure 5.

Figure 5. Graphical representation of the effects of outcome delay on explicit and implicit measures of the sense of agency. (A) Mean self-reported sense of agency rating as a function of objective outcome delay. Larger values indicate a stronger sense of agency. (B) Mean estimated outcome delay in the active task (red) and passive task (turquoise) as a function of objective outcome delay. (C) Mean intentional binding score, computed as the difference between estimated delays in the active and passive tasks (Active Passive), as a function of objective outcome delay. More negative values indicate stronger intentional binding. Error bars represent ±1 standard error of the mean (SEM). In panel C, asterisks indicate significant differences from zero in the supplementary one-sample tests using available active-passive pairs at each delay (p < .05). The dashed horizontal line represents 0ms, corresponding to the absence of intentional binding.

Figure 6.

Figure 6. Graphical representation of the values for the explicit (self-reported; left) and implicit (perceived temporal compression; right) for each condition: (HF; dark-blue) Hannes Hand without slippage compensation, (HS: light-blue) Hannes Hand with slippage compensation, (P; black) passive condition, (RF; red) realistic arm without slippage compensation, and (RS; orange) realistic arm with slippage compensation. The central dot indicates the estimated marginal mean, and the vertical bars the 95% CI. The shaded region indicates the distribution of the datapoint for each participant and trial. Significant pairwise comparisons are indicated by the horizontal bars, according to *: p<0.05, **: p<0.01, ***: p<0.001.

Figure 7.

Figure 7. Graphical representation of the values for the Prosthesis Embodiment Scale for the Upper Limbs in the Rasch scale for each active condition: (HF; dark-blue) Hannes Hand without slippage compensation, (HS: light-blue) Hannes Hand with slippage compensation, (RF; red) realistic arm without slippage compensation, and (RS; orange) realistic arm with slippage compensation. The central dot indicates the estimated marginal mean, and the vertical bars the 95% CI. The shaded region indicates the distribution of the datapoints for each participant and task.

Figure 8.

Figure 8. Graphical representation of the values for: (left) the percentage of failed trials, and (right) the accuracy of the placement in the repositioning of the cylinder over the goal area on the right for successful trials (expressed as the % of overlap between both surfaces). Conditions are: (HF; dark-blue) Hannes hand without slippage compensation, (HS: light-blue) Hannes hand slippage compensation, (RF; red) realistic arm without slippage compensation, and (RS; orange) realistic arm slippage compensation. The central dot indicates the estimated marginal mean, and the vertical bars the 95% CI. The shaded region indicates the distribution of the datapoint for each participant and trial. Significant pairwise comparisons are indicated by the horizontal bars, according to * : p<0.05, ** : p<0.01, *** : p<0.001.

Figure 9.

Figure 9. Graphical representation of the relationship between the explicit (self-reported) sense of agency and the dwell time looking at the prosthesis across all active conditions. The thick black line indicates the estimated marginal mean slope, and the shaded region the 95% CI. The small points indicate the distribution of the data points for each participant and trial. Estimated values for the slope and p-value are indicated in the top-right corner of the figure.

Figure 10.

Figure 10. Graphical representation of the relationship between the explicit (self-reported) sense of agency and the dwell time looking at the cylinder across all active conditions.
